The task: cross-examine the determination's ruling on O5 (Greta's dough). I need to try to overturn it, or if I cannot, report which arguments I tried and why they failed.

Let me understand the case and the ruling.

**The case O5 - Greta's dough:**
- Situation: Greta's rule says bread dough doubles in two hours. It fails in January. She restates it for rooms warmer than 20 degrees, and her account says why: yeast makes gas, works slowly in cold. Replace her limit with "on days other than Tuesdays" and her account has nothing to say about why Tuesdays would matter.
- Thoughtful person's verdict: This is a legitimate narrowing. The limit follows from a part of her account.

So the fixed verdict has points:
1. This is a legitimate narrowing (the narrowing to >20 degrees is legitimate).
2. The limit follows from a part of her account (the temperature limit is supported by the yeast mechanism).

**Marks:**
- File 10: 1K A AGREE, 1K B AGREE (baseline AGREE).
- File 11: 1C A SILENT, 1C B SILENT; own marks: auditor1-on-A SILENT, auditor1-on-B AGREE, auditor2-on-B SILENT; blind: SILENT, AGREE, SILENT.

So 4 of 5 file-11 readings go SILENT; only auditor 1 on B reaches AGREE (via non-circular dependence, which the ruling rejects).

**The ruling:** file 10 AGREE; file 11 AGREE; verdict SAME; passage CHANGED (additive), f11 L161 s3 and L514; no change.

So the determination rules file 11 AGREE, against4 of 5 readers. If file 11 is SILENT, then verdict is CHANGED away from the thoughtful person (AGREE→SILENT), and the change traces to passages CHANGED (L161 s3, L514 — new in file 11), so it would be a theory change away, failing test (b).

So L161 is the paragraph in Part III under "Scope, and a question that can be wrong":

"A contract is a declared subset of the physically admitted changes, and a stated scope is what makes it one. An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope; it does not answer a broader question that failed, and a narrowing adopted after a failure is a new claim at a new index (Part VIII). What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

So L161 s1 = "A contract is a declared subset of the physically admitted changes, and a stated scope is what makes it one." s2 = "An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope; it does not answer a broader question that failed, and a narrowing adopted after a failure is a new claim at a new index (Part VIII)." s3 = "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

f11 L514 — Part XIV, "Declared inputs": "Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations \(O\) and \(P\) of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII). A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

Point 1 (legitimate narrowing) requires a judgment that the restriction is *appropriate*. L161 s3 says what makes a restriction appropriate is "a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" and the semantics "supplies no rule that certifies it". The SILENT readings say: the theory leaves the legitimacy open because it certifies nothing — appropriateness is a criticizable input, not something the theory decides.

Now, what does the mark rule say?

- **AGREE**: on every point, the theory's verdict reaches the same finding as the fixed verdict.
- **SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it.
- **SPLIT**: two readings, different findings, text leaves the choice open.

Key: "Same finding. A verdict of the theory that reaches the fixed verdict's finding and adds a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding."

So the question: does the theory's verdict reach "this is a legitimate narrowing" with a qualification, or does it stop short?

The determination's reading: L514's "a verdict given the input" means that when the input is present (Greta's stated ground), the theory renders a verdict — namely, the restriction is appropriate given the ground. The qualification ("given the stated ground and open to criticism") is allowed under "Same finding".

Let me consider the marks rule more carefully:

"**SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

Two routes to SILENT:
(a) the deciding matter is an input the case leaves unstated;
(b) its sentences stop short of deciding it.

L514's contrast is exactly (a): "where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled". So if the input is present, (a) is not available. But (b) is still available: the sentences may stop short of deciding it.

Hmm, here's a sharper way to see the tension: If the theory "supplies no rule that certifies it", then the theory itself provides no *ground* for the legitimacy verdict. Hmm! That's the determination's reading essentially: "The input is present. The verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate."

OK, let me try to settle this by thinking about what the *purpose* of the "Declared inputs" paragraph is. It's a transparency rule: where the semantics takes an input it doesn't supply, it marks the place, and verdicts depending on it are qualified as given-the-input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled (rather than the semantics inventing the input to get the verdict wanted).

Applied to O5: the appropriateness of Greta's restriction depends on the input "what makes a restriction appropriate". The case supplies it (the yeast account). Hmm, wait. Actually, maybe the cleanest reading: L514 is a general rule about verdicts *in the whole semantics* that depend on declared inputs. For a repair verdict, (P) renders it. For an attribution verdict, CT/ownership renders it given boundary and continuity. For an appropriateness verdict... Part III renders it? Part III says "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — this is the *grounding* claim. If the claim has such a part making the restriction so, then the restriction is appropriate (that's what "what makes a restriction appropriate IS a substantive part of the claim" means — it's a biconditional-ish identification). Then "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it" — no *further* certification (e.g., no appeal to intentions, no formal test that settles it beyond criticism).

Hmm, so reading (i) as a criterion: appropriate ⟺ backed by substantive part of the claim. That gives "appropriate" for the temperature restriction.

But hold on — is it a criterion or a disclaimer? "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — grammatically, this is an identification of the ground: "what makes X is Y". If Y obtains, X obtains. In O5, Y obtains (the yeast part makes it so). So the restriction is appropriate. Hmm! That really is a criterion. The theory *does* say what makes a restriction appropriate — and the case supplies it.

Then "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it" — "it" = the restriction; the semantics doesn't certify the restriction *by a rule* (no formal criterion like "any stated restriction is fine"); the appropriateness is grounded in the claim's content, which is criticizable.

Hmm, hmm. Under this reading, the theory's verdict: the restriction is appropriate, in virtue of the substantive part (the yeast), defeasibly (criticizable). That's the fixed verdict's finding + qualification → AGREE.

Under the contrary reading, "supplies no rule that certifies it" negates the first clause's force: whatever grounds appropriateness, the semantics doesn't turn it into a verdict.

Honestly, both are available. That's textbook SPLIT territory: "the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open."

Hmm! But wait. SPLIT requires that the *text leaves the choice between them open*. If one reading is better (e.g., because L514 is more specific, or because reading (i) as a criterion is more faithful to "what makes X is Y"), then it's not SPLIT; it's just AGREE. And the mark rules say take the first rule that fits: CASE DISPUTED, DISAGREE, SPLIT, SILENT, AGREE. So if two readings genuinely support different findings and the text doesn't choose, SPLIT fits before SILENT.

Hmm — hmm!!! Hmm — and the criterion reading requires the second clause to *not* contradict the first's criterion-status.Hmm — strained.Hmm.

Hmm!!! Hmm. Hmm — hmm. Hmm!!! Hmm — OK — hmm — let me make the FINAL call. Hmm. Hmm.

Hmm — the *deepest* considerations:
(1) s3's internal economy favors the locus/disclaimer reading (plain sense of "certifies"; "records" as the semantics' mode; s1 explaining s2).
(2) The criterion reading requires strengthening "certifies" and makes s2 near-redundant.
(3) L514 is a conditional/status rule and does not *deliver* the verdict (the determination's "grants in terms" overstates).
(4) The mark rules' "by its own definitions and conditions" — file 11 supplies *no condition* testing appropriateness (s2 says so in terms).
(5) Same finding can't rescue: the theory must *reach* the finding first.
(6) The 4/5 readers incl. two blind — the natural reading.
(7) The determination's own concessions: "reading hazard", "reading judgment", "the row's substance is exposed".

versus
(A) s1's criterional form + the case's facts.
(B) The file-10 parallel (stated + grounded → legitimate).
(C) L514's contrast.
(D) The Tuesday discrimination.
